<div class="ltx_page_main">

<div class="ltx_page_content">

# ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents

<div class="ltx_authors">

<span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author">
<span class="ltx_personname">Qirui Mi </span></span>
<span class="ltx_author_before">  </span><span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author">
<span class="ltx_personname">Zhijian Ma </span></span>
<span class="ltx_author_before">  </span><span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author">
<span class="ltx_personname">Mengyue Yang </span></span>
<span class="ltx_author_before">  </span><span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author">
<span class="ltx_personname">Haoxuan Li </span></span>
<span class="ltx_author_before">  </span><span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author">
<span class="ltx_personname">Yisen Wang </span></span>
<span class="ltx_author_before">  </span><span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author">
<span class="ltx_personname">Haifeng Zhang </span></span>
<span class="ltx_author_before">  </span><span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author">
<span class="ltx_personname">Jun Wang </span></span>

</div>

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract

LLM-driven agents demonstrate strong performance in sequential
decision-making but often rely on on-the-fly reasoning, re-deriving
solutions even in recurring scenarios. This insufficient experience
reuse leads to computational redundancy and execution instability. To
bridge this gap, we propose
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ProcMEM</span>, a framework that
enables agents to autonomously learn procedural memory from interaction
experiences without parameter updates. By formalizing a
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Skill-MDP</span>, ProcMEM
transforms passive episodic narratives into executable Skills defined by
activation, execution, and termination conditions to ensure
executability. To achieve reliable reusability without capability
degradation, we introduce
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Non-Parametric PPO</span>, which
leverages semantic gradients for high-quality candidate generation and a
PPO Gate for robust Skill verification. Through score-based maintenance,
ProcMEM sustains compact, high-quality procedural memory. Experimental
results across in-domain, cross-task, and cross-agent scenarios
demonstrate that ProcMEM achieves
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">superior reuse rates and
significant performance gains</span> with extreme memory compression.
Visualized evolutionary trajectories and Skill distributions further
reveal how ProcMEM transparently accumulates, refines, and reuses
procedural knowledge to facilitate long-term autonomy.

</div>

<div class="ltx_keywords">

Machine Learning, ICML

</div>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction

<figure id="S1.F1" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2602.01869/assets/fig/problem_fig.png" id="S1.F1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="539"
height="302" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S1.F1.3.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 1</span>: </span><span
id="S1.F1.4.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> <span
id="S1.F1.4.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Episodic memory versus
procedural memory in LLM-driven agents.</span> Episodic memory retrieves
past interactions for reference, requiring inference-heavy reasoning at
decision time. Procedural memory encodes reusable executable Skills that
directly map situations to actions, enabling efficient experience reuse.
</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Large Language Model (LLM)-driven agents have shown strong performance
in complex sequential
decision-making (Park<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="Generative agents: interactive simulacra of human behavior">2023</a>;
Shinn<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reflexion: language agents with verbal reinforcement learning">2023</a>).
However, this performance is often driven by <span id="S1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">on-the-fly reasoning</span>, where agents
interpret prompts, observations, and feedback in real-time to generate
solutions (Wei<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib54" class="ltx_ref"
title="Chain-of-thought prompting elicits reasoning in large language models">2022</a>;
Yao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref"
title="React: synergizing reasoning and acting in language models">2022</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref"
title="Tree of thoughts: deliberate problem solving with large language models">2023</a>).
Even in recurring situations, they typically redo the full reasoning
process from scratch, treating each interaction as an unseen problem.
This insufficient experience reuse results in substantial computational
redundancy and increases the risk of error accumulation in long-horizon
scenarios, eventually leading to lower reliability in
execution (Liu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref"
title="Lost in the middle: how language models use long contexts">2024</a>;
Press<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref"
title="Measuring and narrowing the compositionality gap in language models">2023</a>).

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

To incorporate interaction experience, existing work broadly falls into
two paradigms. <span id="S1.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Parametric methods</span>, such as
Reinforcement Learning (RL), attempt to encode experience into model
parameters (Sutton<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforcement learning: an introduction">1998</a>;
Ouyang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref"
title="Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback">2022</a>).
While effective in specific domains, these approaches face high training
costs, risks of catastrophic forgetting, and a narrowing of
general-purpose
capabilities (Kirkpatrick<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Overcoming catastrophic forgetting in neural networks">2017</a>).
Alternatively, <span id="S1.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">non-parametric methods</span> improve
behavior at inference time without updating the base LLM, most commonly
via external memory (Hu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memory in the age of ai agents">2025</a>). These agents store
diverse forms of experience in external memory, including past
trajectories (Lewis<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref"
title="Retrieval-augmented generation for knowledge-intensive nlp tasks">2020</a>;
Rajesh<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref"
title="Beyond fact retrieval: episodic memory for rag with generative semantic workspaces">2025</a>),
distilled reflections (Xu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref"
title="A-mem: agentic memory for llm agents">2025</a>;
Zhao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref"
title="Expel: llm agents are experiential learners">2024</a>), and
structured graphs (Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref"
title="G-memory: tracing hierarchical memory for multi-agent systems">2025a</a>;
Xia<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref"
title="From experience to strategy: empowering llm agents with trainable graph memory">2025</a>)
or workflows (Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref"
title="Agent workflow memory">2024</a>). At decision time, they retrieve
stored experiences to condition the LLM’s reasoning and improve
performance, typically without updating the base LLM. However, despite
their effectiveness, these methods predominantly operate as forms of
episodic memory (Cohen and Squire, <a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref"
title="Preserved learning and retention of pattern-analyzing skill in amnesia: dissociation of knowing how and knowing that">1980</a>),
storing and retrieving past experiences as “history books” to be
consulted (Fig. <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>). Even with large memory, the
agent still has to spend its limited context window interpreting
retrieved cases and re-deriving solutions, effectively returning to the
inference-heavy loop. Inspired by <span id="S1.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">procedural memory</span> in human
cognition, an implicit system that directly maps situations to action
patterns (Squire, <a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memory systems of the brain: a brief history and current perspective">2004</a>);
once acquired, it enables the automatic execution of Skills without
conscious re-derivation (Anderson, <a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref"
title="Acquisition of cognitive skill.">1982</a>). While frameworks like
Claude Agent Skills (Anthropic,
<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref" title="Agent skills">2025</a>) reuse
manually encoded procedures, this work investigates <span id="S1.p2.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">how LLM agents can
autonomously learn reusable procedural Skills from interaction
experience for future decision-making.</span>

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">

However, establishing reusable procedural memory faces three fundamental
obstacles: <span id="S1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">C1:
Executability.</span> Interaction experience is often stored as passive
episodic narratives describing past events rather than active decision
procedures that can be directly instantiated at runtime.
<span id="S1.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">C2:
Reusability.</span> The challenge lies in ensuring that stored
procedures can be reliably invoked and reused in future tasks while
providing a consistent performance gain. <span id="S1.p3.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">C3: Non-Parametric Optimization.</span>
The difficulty lies in learning reusable procedural memory through
non-parametric methods while preserving the agent’s general-purpose
capabilities.

</div>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">

To address these challenges, we propose <span id="S1.p4.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ProcMEM</span>, a framework designed to
learn reusable <span id="S1.p4.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">pro</span>cedural <span id="S1.p4.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">mem</span>ory from interaction experience
without parameter updates. First, ProcMEM formalizes procedural units as
Skills consisting of Activation Conditions, Execution Procedures, and
Termination Conditions. By constructing a <span id="S1.p4.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Skill-MDP</span>, the agent selects and
reuses these executable procedures (Skills) for decision-making to
ensure executability (<span id="S1.p4.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">C1</span>). To achieve reliable
reusability (<span id="S1.p4.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">C2</span>) through non-parametric
optimization (<span id="S1.p4.1.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">C3</span>), we introduce
<span id="S1.p4.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Non-Parametric
PPO</span>. This mechanism leverages <span id="S1.p4.1.9"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">semantic gradients</span> extracted
from batch trajectories to propose refined Skill candidates, while a
PPO-style Trust-Region Verification (PPO Gate) ensures the selection of
high-quality Skills for inclusion in the procedural memory. Furthermore,
an online scoring mechanism filters out redundant or low-quality
procedures. Experimental results demonstrate that ProcMEM achieves
<span id="S1.p4.1.10" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">superior reuse
rates, significant performance gains, and extreme memory
compression</span> compared to baselines across in-domain, cross-task,
and cross-agent scenarios (Table <a href="#S4.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ 4.3 Score-Based Skill Pool Maintenance ‣ 4 Non-Parametric PPO for Skill Evolution ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>,
<a href="#S4.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 4.3 Score-Based Skill Pool Maintenance ‣ 4 Non-Parametric PPO for Skill Evolution ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>). Ablation studies confirm
that Semantic Gradients and the PPO Gate are indispensable for
generating and verifying high-quality skills, while online scoring
preserves long-term evolutionary gains
(Table <a href="#S5.T3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 3 ‣ 5.3 Why Does ProcMEM Work? ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>,
Fig. <a href="#S5.F3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 3 ‣ 5.3 Why Does ProcMEM Work? ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>). Finally, visualized
evolutionary trajectories (Fig. <a href="#S5.F4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 4 ‣ 5.4 How Does ProcMEM Evolve and Reuse? ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>) and Skill distribution
(Fig. <a href="#S5.F5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 5 ‣ 5.4 How Does ProcMEM Evolve and Reuse? ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>) reveal how ProcMEM’s
procedural memory is transparently constructed and reused to facilitate
long-term autonomy. <span id="S1.p4.1.11"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Our core contributions are
three-fold:</span>

- <span id="S1.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Procedural
  Memory Formalization</span>: We introduce the Skill-MDP, transitioning
  LLM agents from episodic narratives to reusable Skills.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Non-Parametric PPO</span>: We propose a
  parameter-free optimization mechanism leveraging
  <span id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Semantic
  Gradients</span>, a <span id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1.3"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">PPO Gate</span>, and
  <span id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1.4"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">score-based maintenance</span> to
  evolve high-quality skills without weight updates.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Superior
  reuse rates and performance gain</span> across diverse scenarios with
  extreme memory compression.<span id="footnote1"
  class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">1</span>Code
  is available at:  
  <a href="https://github.com/Miracle1207/ProcMEM"
  class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter"
  target="_blank">https://github.com/Miracle1207/ProcMEM</a></span></span></span>

  </div>

</div>

<figure id="S1.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2602.01869/assets/x1.png" id="S1.F2.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="705"
height="254" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S1.F2.23.7.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 2</span>: </span><span
id="S1.F2.12.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">Overview of the ProcMEM framework.<span
id="S1.F2.12.6.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> </span>(Left)
Skill-MDP:<span id="S1.F2.11.5.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> The
agent selects a Skill <span class="math inline"><em>ω</em></span> based
on state <span
class="math inline"><em>s</em><sub><em>t</em></sub></span> and
activation conditions. A frozen LLM executes <span
class="math inline"><em>ω</em></span> into primitive actions <span
class="math inline"><em>a</em><sub><em>t</em></sub></span> over multiple
steps until termination. Post-episode trajectories <span
class="math inline">𝒯</span> are stored in a buffer. </span>(Middle)
Procedural Memory:<span id="S1.F2.12.6.8"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Skills are dynamically managed via
<span id="S1.F2.12.6.8.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">refinement</span>, <span
id="S1.F2.12.6.8.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">generation</span>,
and <span id="S1.F2.12.6.8.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">score-based pruning</span> to maintain
pool quality. </span>(Right) Non-Parametric PPO:<span id="S1.F2.12.6.9"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Evolution proceeds in two stages: ①
</span>Semantic Gradient<span id="S1.F2.12.6.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium">: Derives and aggregates per-trajectory
gradients through hindsight attribution to generate candidates <span
class="math inline"><em>ω</em><sup>′</sup></span>. ② </span>PPO
Gate<span id="S1.F2.12.6.10" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium">: Filters
candidates via trust-region verification, admitting only the
best-performing valid candidate into the Skill
pool.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Related Work

<div id="S2.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">(A comprehensive
literature review is shown in Appendix <a href="#A1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix A Full Related Works ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A</span></a>.)</span> <span id="S2.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Learning from Interaction.</span> LLM
agents improve decision-making via *parametric fine-tuning*, such as
reinforcement learning (Ouyang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref"
title="Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback">2022</a>;
Rafailov<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref"
title="Direct preference optimization: your language model is secretly a reward model">2023</a>;
Guo<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref"
title="DeepSeek-r1 incentivizes reasoning in llms through reinforcement learning">2025</a>),
or *non-parametric adaptation*. While parametric updates yield strong
performance, they often incur high computational costs and risk
catastrophic forgetting or over-specialization
(Ziegler<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref"
title="Fine-tuning language models from human preferences">2020</a>;
Shi<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref"
title="Continual learning of large language models: a comprehensive survey">2025a</a>;
Luo<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref"
title="An empirical study of catastrophic forgetting in large language models during continual fine-tuning">2025</a>),
driving the shift toward memory-augmented agents as a more efficient,
non-parametric alternative.

</div>

<div id="S2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Memory-Augmented LLM
Agents.</span> Existing frameworks primarily differ in experience
representation: (i) <span id="S2.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Episodic Trajectories:</span> Storing raw
trajectories for case-based reasoning
(Park<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="Generative agents: interactive simulacra of human behavior">2023</a>);
(ii) <span id="S2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Abstracted
Knowledge:</span> Distilling experience into summaries
(Yang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref"
title="Learning on the job: an experience-driven self-evolving agent for long-horizon tasks">2025</a>),
high-level principles (Wu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref"
title="EvolveR: self-evolving llm agents through an experience-driven lifecycle">2025</a>;
Agrawal<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref"
title="Gepa: reflective prompt evolution can outperform reinforcement learning">2025</a>;
Cai<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref"
title="Flex: continuous agent evolution via forward learning from experience">2025</a>),
or failure-derived insights (Zhao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref"
title="Expel: llm agents are experiential learners">2024</a>); (iii)
<span id="S2.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Structured &
Compressed Memory:</span> Organizing experience into graphs
(Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref"
title="G-memory: tracing hierarchical memory for multi-agent systems">2025a</a>;
Jimenez Gutierrez<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref"
title="Hipporag: neurobiologically inspired long-term memory for large language models">2024</a>;
Rezazadeh<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref"
title="From isolated conversations to hierarchical schemas: dynamic tree memory representation for llms">2024</a>;
Xia<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref"
title="From experience to strategy: empowering llm agents with trainable graph memory">2025</a>),
dense vectors (Das<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref"
title="Larimar: large language models with episodic memory control">2024</a>;
Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memgen: weaving generative latent memory for self-evolving agents">2025b</a>),
or dynamic knowledge snippets (Asai<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref"
title="Self-rag: learning to retrieve, generate, and critique through self-reflection">2024</a>;
Shi<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref"
title="Look back to reason forward: revisitable memory for long-context llm agents">2025b</a>;
Zhou<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref"
title="MEM1: learning to synergize memory and reasoning for efficient long-horizon agents">2025b</a>);
(iv) <span id="S2.p2.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Workflow:</span> Maintaining explicit
task-completion paths (Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref"
title="Agent workflow memory">2024</a>). Skill-centric and procedural
frameworks (Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Voyager: an open-ended embodied agent with large language models">2023</a>;
Tan<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Cradle: empowering foundation agents towards general computer control">2024</a>;
Zhu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref"
title="Ghost in the minecraft: generally capable agents for open-world environments via large language models with text-based knowledge and memory">2023</a>;
Sumers<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Cognitive architectures for language agents">2023</a>;
Han<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Legomem: modular procedural memory for multi-agent llm systems for workflow automation">2025</a>;
Fang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memp: exploring agent procedural memory">2025</a>) leverage
executable logic, yet robust reusability remains non-trivial. To bridge
the gap between storage and reusability, we propose <span id="S2.p2.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ProcMEM</span> to learn procedural memory
for efficient, autonomous long-term execution.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Reusable Procedural Units: Skills

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this section, we introduce *Skills* as reusable procedural units
integrated into the decision-making process of LLM agents. We define a
<span id="S3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Skill</span> as a
temporally extended procedural units specifying: (1)
<span id="S3.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">when</span> it
should be activated, (2) <span id="S3.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">how</span> the agent should execute a
sequence of actions, and (3) <span id="S3.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">when</span> control should return.

</div>

<div id="S3.p2" class="ltx_para">

Unlike human procedural memory (Cohen and Squire,
<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref"
title="Preserved learning and retention of pattern-analyzing skill in amnesia: dissociation of knowing how and knowing that">1980</a>;
Squire, <a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memory systems of the brain: a brief history and current perspective">2004</a>),
which is largely implicit, our Skills are currently represented in an
explicit, readable form, similar to systems such as Claude Agent
Skills (Anthropic,
<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref" title="Agent skills">2025</a>).
While explicit in representation, Skills can be hidden at the system
level during execution; directions toward implicit procedural
representations are discussed in
Appendix <span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_label ltx_ref_self">LABEL:sec:appendix_procedural_memory</span>.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.1 </span>Problem Formulation

<div id="S3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We formulate the LLM agent’s decision-making process as a
<span id="S3.SS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Skill-augmented
Markov Decision Process (Skill-MDP)</span>. The Skill-MDP extends the
classical Markov Decision Process (MDP) by introducing a *dynamic Skill
pool* $`\Omega`$, which explicitly represents the agent’s procedural
memory and organizes decision making around the selection and execution
of reusable procedural Skills. Formally, a Skill-MDP is defined as a
tuple

|     |                                                                   |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                                                   
       ``` math                                                           
       \mathcal{M}_{\Omega}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\Omega,P,R,\gamma),  
       ```                                                                |     |

where $`\mathcal{S}`$ denotes the semantic state space and
$`\mathcal{A}`$ denotes the primitive action space, both represented in
natural language; $`\Omega=\{\omega^{(1)},\ldots,\omega^{(K)}\}`$
denotes the pool of available Skills, which serves as the agent’s
procedural memory, with $`K`$ Skills stored; $`P`$ and $`R`$ are the
state transition and reward functions, respectively, and
$`\gamma\in(0,1]`$ is the discount factor.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

In a Skill-MDP, at each time step $`t`$, the agent first selects a Skill
via a Skill-selection policy $`\mu`$, conditioned on the current state
$`s_{t}`$ and the current Skill pool $`\Omega`$:

|     |                                             |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                             
       ``` math                                     
       \omega_{t}\sim\mu(\omega\mid s_{t},\Omega).  
       ```                                          |     |

Conditioned on the active Skill $`\omega_{t}`$, the agent then generates
a primitive action using an LLM-driven action policy:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
a_{t}\sim\pi_{\text{LLM}}(a\mid s_{t},\omega_{t}).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(1)</span> |

Accordingly, the hierarchical policy over Skills and primitive actions
factorizes as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\pi_{\Omega}(\omega_{t},a_{t}\mid s_{t})\;=\;\mu(\omega_{t}\mid s_{t},\Omega)\,\pi_{\text{LLM}}(a_{t}\mid s_{t},\omega_{t}),
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(2)</span> |

After executing $`a_{t}`$, the agent receives reward $`r_{t}`$ and
transitions to next state $`s_{t+1}`$ until the horizon $`T`$ is
reached. While Eq. (<a href="#S3.E2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Equation 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Formulation ‣ 3 Reusable Procedural Units: Skills ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>) shares a similar
factorization with memory-augmented agents (e.g.,
Memento (Zhou<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memento: fine-tuning llm agents without fine-tuning llms">2025a</a>)),
which typically optimize the retrieval policy $`\mu`$, our work focuses
on autonomously evolving the Skill pool $`\Omega`$.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Optimization
Objective.</span> We aim to optimize the agent’s decision-making
performance under the hierarchical policy $`\pi_{\Omega}`$, measured by
the expected cumulative discounted return:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\max_{\pi_{\Omega}}\;\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T}\gamma^{t}r_{t}\right].
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(3)</span> |

In this paper, we focus on learning the Skill pool $`\Omega`$, while
keeping the LLM policy and the Skill-selection mechanism fixed. We next
specify the internal structure of a Skill, the basic reusable procedural
unit in the Skill-MDP.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.2 </span>Skill Structure

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We define a *Skill* $`\omega\in\Omega`$ as a reusable, natural-language
procedural unit that specifies *when* to activate, *how* to act while
active, and *when* to return control. This enables Skills to be reused
across similar states. Formally, a Skill is defined as the tuple

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\omega=\langle\mathcal{I}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\beta_{\omega}\rangle,
``` |  |

representing its activation condition, execution procedure, and
termination condition.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(1) Activation
Condition $`\mathcal{I}_{\omega}`$.</span> The activation condition
specifies when a Skill is invoked. Rather than a learned classifier in
latent space, $`\mathcal{I}_{\omega}`$ is a natural-language description
of observable context patterns where the Skill applies. During decision
making, the Skill-selection policy $`\mu`$ uses $`\mathcal{I}_{\omega}`$
to select Skills based on the current state or interaction history.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(2) Execution
Procedure $`\pi_{\omega}`$.</span> The execution procedure
$`\pi_{\omega}`$ specifies an ordered sequence of actions to be executed
while the Skill $`\omega`$ is active, expressed as in natural language.
At each time step $`t`$, the LLM generates a primitive action
conditioned on the current state $`s_{t}`$ and $`\pi_{\omega}`$
(Eq. (<a href="#S3.E1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Equation 1 ‣ 3.1 Problem Formulation ‣ 3 Reusable Procedural Units: Skills ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>)), enabling the agent to reuse
procedural steps without re-deriving deliberative reasoning from
scratch.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(3) Termination
Condition $`\beta_{\omega}`$.</span> The termination condition specifies
when the execution of a Skill should end. Like the activation condition,
$`\beta_{\omega}`$ is expressed in natural language and evaluated on the
current state:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\beta_{\omega}(s_{t})=1\;\;\text{iff }s_{t}\text{ satisfies }\beta_{\omega}.
``` |  |

If $`\beta_{\omega}(s_{t})=1`$, the Skill $`\omega`$ terminates and
$`\mu`$ selects the next Skill; otherwise, it remains active.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p5" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS2.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Example.</span>
We illustrate with an example Skill:

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p6" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS2.p6.3"
class="ltx_inline-block ltx_framed ltx_framed_rectangle"
style="--ltx-border-color:#000000;"> <span id="S3.SS2.p6.3.4"
class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS2.p6.3.4.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Name:</span> StrategicPlanning</span>
<span id="S3.SS2.p6.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS2.p6.1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Activation Condition
$`\mathcal{I}_{\omega}`$:</span> *When the task begins and no prior
information or feedback is available.*</span> <span id="S3.SS2.p6.2.2"
class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS2.p6.2.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Execution Procedure
$`\pi_{\omega}`$:</span></span> <span id="S3.SS2.p6.3.5"
class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS2.p6.3.5.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Step 1: Establish an initial hypothesis
space based on the task constraints.</span></span>
<span id="S3.SS2.p6.3.6" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS2.p6.3.6.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Step 2: Generate a diverse exploratory
action that maximally reduces uncertainty.</span></span>
<span id="S3.SS2.p6.3.3" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.SS2.p6.3.3.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Termination Condition
$`\beta_{\omega}`$:</span> *Terminate after the first exploratory action
is executed and feedback is observed.*</span> </span>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.3 </span>Skill Selection

<div id="S3.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

At each decision step $`t`$, the agent maintains a Skill pool
$`\Omega_{t}`$. A new Skill is selected by the Skill-selection policy
$`\mu`$ only upon termination of the current Skill. In that case,
$`\mu`$ selects a Skill $`\omega_{t}\in\Omega_{t}`$ based on the current
state $`s_{t}`$ and the pool $`\Omega_{t}`$. We present two simple
instantiations of $`\mu`$.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(i) Selection by
Similarity.</span> This mechanism selects the Skill whose activation
condition best matches the state $`s_{t}`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\omega_{t}=\arg\max\nolimits_{\omega\in\Omega_{t}}\mathrm{Sim}(s_{t},\mathcal{I}_{\omega}),
``` |  |

where $`\mathrm{Sim}(s_{t},\mathcal{I}_{\omega})`$ measures the
similarity between the current state and the activation condition
$`\mathcal{I}_{\omega}`$. The similarity function
$`\mathrm{Sim(\cdot,\cdot)}`$ can be implemented via cosine similarity
over embeddings, or LLM as judge.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS3.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(ii) Selection
by Value.</span> We also support value-based selection to prioritize
Skills with higher expected return. We first form a candidate set of the
top-$`k`$ Skills by similarity:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\Omega_{t}^{(k)}=\operatorname{TopK}\nolimits_{\omega\in\Omega_{t}}\mathrm{Sim}(s_{t},\mathcal{I}_{\omega}),
``` |  |

where $`\Omega_{t}^{(k)}\subseteq\Omega_{t}`$ and
$`|\Omega_{t}^{(k)}|=k`$. We then select the Skill with the highest
estimated value in this set:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\omega_{t}=\arg\max\nolimits_{\omega\in\Omega_{t}^{(k)}}Q(s_{t},\omega).
``` |  |

Here $`Q(s_{t},\omega)`$ denotes an estimate of the expected return
obtained by invoking Skill $`\omega`$ from state $`s_{t}`$.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p4" class="ltx_para">

These mechanisms are simple instantiations of $`\mu`$ and can be
replaced by more advanced Skill retrieval policies, e.g., RL-based
methods (Zhou<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memento: fine-tuning llm agents without fine-tuning llms">2025a</a>;
Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="MemRL: self-evolving agents via runtime reinforcement learning on episodic memory">2026</a>).
Our focus lies on Skill reuse and evolution.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.4 </span>Skill Pool Evolution

<div id="S3.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

The Skill pool is learned from interaction experience and evolves over
time. In our framework, the pool is updated using trajectories generated
under the current policy. Given a batch of trajectories
$`\mathcal{T}^{(B)}`$ collected under the Skill-augmented policy
$`\pi_{\Omega}`$, we define a Skill pool evolution operator
$`\mathcal{E}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\Omega_{\text{new}}\;=\;\mathcal{E}(\Omega_{\text{old}},\mathcal{T}^{(B)}),
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(4)</span> |

which synthesizes new Skills, refines existing Skills, and prunes those
that underperform empirically.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

This paper focuses on Skill pool evolution. The LLM action policy
$`\pi_{\text{LLM}}`$ and the Skill-selection policy $`\mu`$ are kept
fixed, and learning proceeds through repeated application of
$`\mathcal{E}`$. Under this setting, optimizing
Eq. (<a href="#S3.E3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Equation 3 ‣ 3.1 Problem Formulation ‣ 3 Reusable Procedural Units: Skills ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>) reduces to optimizing the
evolution operator $`\mathcal{E}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\max_{\mathcal{E}}\;\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi_{\Omega^{*}}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T}\gamma^{t}r_{t}\right],\quad\text{where }\Omega^{*}=\mathcal{E}^{(N)}(\Omega_{0}).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(5)</span> |

Here, $`\mathcal{E}^{(N)}`$ denotes $`N`$ successive applications of
$`\mathcal{E}`$, each using newly collected experience.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Non-Parametric PPO for Skill Evolution

<div id="S4.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this section, we present a Proximal Policy Optimization
(PPO)-inspired non-parametric method for Skill pool evolution, which we
refer to as *Non-Parametric PPO*. This method leverages PPO-style
trust-region principles to realize reliable Skill pool evolution as
defined in Eq. (<a href="#S3.E4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Equation 4 ‣ 3.4 Skill Pool Evolution ‣ 3 Reusable Procedural Units: Skills ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>), without updating any LLM
parameters.

</div>

<div id="S4.p2" class="ltx_para">

Standard PPO (Schulman<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref"
title="Proximal policy optimization algorithms">2017</a>) improves a
parameterized stochastic policy through gradient-based optimization of a
clipped surrogate objective. In contrast, our Non-Parametric PPO
replaces parameter updates with Skill refinement, and consists of two
components: (1) generating candidate Skills via <span id="S4.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">semantic gradients</span> extracted
from trajectories, and (2) accepting candidates only if they satisfy a
<span id="S4.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">PPO-style
trust-region verification</span> under the frozen LLM policy. The
complete procedure is shown in Algorithm <a href="#alg1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Algorithm 1 ‣ Summary of Appendices ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1 </span>Semantic Gradients

<div id="S4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

To learn without updating LLM parameters, we introduce *Semantic
Gradients* as learning signals that specify how a Skill should be
refined.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS1.p2.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Per-trajectory
semantic gradients.</span> Unlike
TextGrad (Yuksekgonul<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref"
title="Textgrad: automatic” differentiation” via text">2024</a>), which
uses automatic differentiation to optimize static variables for
instantaneous response quality, our *semantic gradients* are designed
for sequential decision making. These gradients provide natural-language
update directions extracted from interaction trajectories, indicating
how a Skill’s activation, execution, and termination conditions should
be refined via *hindsight attribution*. Consider a Skill
$`\omega=\langle\mathcal{I}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},\beta_{\omega}\rangle`$
and trajectories $`\mathcal{T}^{(B)}`$ where it is invoked. For each
trajectory $`\tau_{i}`$, we analyze the segment controlled by $`\omega`$
and attribute the outcome to its activation condition, execution
procedure, or termination condition. This yields a structured semantic
gradient

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
g_{i}=\nabla_{\mathrm{sem}}(\tau_{i},\omega)=\bigl(g_{i}^{(\mathcal{I})},\,g_{i}^{(\pi)},\,g_{i}^{(\beta)}\bigr).
``` |  |

where each component is a natural-language refinement suggestion for the
corresponding Skill component. Intuitively, $`g_{i}`$ serves as a local
update direction for Skill $`\omega`$ induced by trajectory
$`\tau_{i}`$. An example of semantic gradients from our experiments is
given in the Appendix <a href="#A4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix D Case Study: Semantic Gradient Generation ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">D</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS1.p3.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Batch-level
aggregation.</span> Individual trajectories may provide inconsistent
update signals. To obtain a stable learning signal, we aggregate
semantic gradients across the batch:

|     |                                                                   |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                                                   
       ``` math                                                           
       \bar{g}_{\omega}=\text{Aggregate}\bigl(\{g_{i}\}_{i=1}^{B}\bigr),  
       ```                                                                |     |

where $`\text{Aggregate}(\cdot)`$ denotes an LLM-based consolidation
procedure that extracts recurring failure patterns and consistent
refinement suggestions across trajectories, while filtering out
conflicting or trajectory-specific signals. The resulting
$`\bar{g}_{\omega}=(\bar{g}^{(\mathcal{I})},\bar{g}^{(\pi)},\bar{g}^{(\beta)})`$
represents a batch-averaged semantic gradient that captures systematic
weaknesses of Skill $`\omega`$ revealed by experience.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS1.p4.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Semantic Skill
Update.</span> We update Skill $`\omega`$ using the batch-averaged
semantic gradient to obtain a *candidate Skill* $`\omega^{\prime}`$:

|     |                                               |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                               
       ``` math                                       
       \omega^{\prime}=\omega\oplus\bar{g}_{\omega},  
       ```                                            |     |

where $`\oplus`$ denotes an LLM-driven update operation that revises
$`\mathcal{I}_{\omega}`$, $`\pi_{\omega}`$, and $`\beta_{\omega}`$
according to the refinement directions encoded in $`\bar{g}_{\omega}`$,
while preserving the overall Skill structure. This operation plays the
role of a <span id="S4.SS1.p4.7.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">gradient ascent step</span> in
Non-Parametric PPO: <span id="S4.SS1.p4.7.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">instead of updating numerical
parameters, the Skill is updated along a direction intended to improve
expected return, as suggested by aggregated hindsight feedback.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.2 </span>PPO-Style Trust-Region Verification

<div id="S4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Semantic-gradient updates are generated by an LLM from hindsight
feedback. As a result, they may extrapolate beyond the observed
interaction data and introduce hallucinated or behaviorally unstable
Skills. To mitigate this risk, we introduce a PPO-style trust-region
verification step to evaluate each candidate Skill before adding it to
the pool.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

We treat the frozen LLM as the underlying stochastic policy and evaluate
a candidate Skill $`\omega^{\prime}`$ using batch-size trajectories
collected under the previous Skill $`\omega`$. For each timestep $`t`$,
we compute an importance ratio

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\rho_{t}(\omega^{\prime})=\frac{\pi_{\text{LLM}}(a_{t}\mid s_{t},\omega^{\prime})}{\pi_{\text{LLM}}(a_{t}\mid s_{t},\omega)},
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(6)</span> |

which measures how the likelihood of the historical action $`a_{t}`$
would change if the candidate Skill $`\omega^{\prime}`$ were applied
instead of the behavior Skill $`\omega`$ at the same state $`s_{t}`$.
Since we do not train a value function, we estimate advantages using
return-to-go with a running baseline:

$`\textstyle G_{t}=\sum_{k=t}^{T-1}\gamma^{k-t}r_{k},\quad\hat{A}_{t}=G_{t}-\bar{R},`$<span id="S4.SS2.p2.8.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"></span>

where $`\bar{R}`$ is a running baseline used to reduce variance. We
compute a PPO-style clipped surrogate *verification functional*,
hereafter referred to as the <span id="S4.SS2.p2.10.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">PPO Gate</span>, to evaluate the
counterfactual advantage of applying the candidate Skill
$`\omega^{\prime}`$ on historical trajectories:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle L^{\text{CLIP}}(\omega^{\prime})=`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{E}}_{\tau\sim\mathcal{B}}\left[\frac{1}{|\tau|}\sum_{t\in\tau}\min\!\left(\rho_{t}(\omega^{\prime})\hat{A}_{t},\;\operatorname{clip}(\rho_{t}(\omega^{\prime}),1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)\,\hat{A}_{t}\right)\right].`$ |  |

This verification functional favors candidate Skills that assign higher
probability to high-advantage actions observed in past trajectories,
while limiting large deviations from the behavior policy, thereby
enforcing a trust-region constraint.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Best-of-$`N_{c}`$ Selection.</span> Given
$`N_{c}`$ candidates generated via semantic gradients, we compute the
PPO Gate score
$`J(\omega^{\prime})\triangleq L^{\text{CLIP}}(\omega^{\prime})`$ for
each and select the best candidate:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\textstyle\omega_{\text{new}}=\arg\max_{\omega^{\prime}}J(\omega^{\prime}),\quad\text{subject to}\quad J(\omega_{\text{new}})>0.
``` |  |

Since the PPO Gate is based on advantage estimates, a positive score
indicates that the candidate is expected to outperform the previous
Skill under the trust-region constraint, filtering out unreliable or
hallucinated candidates to prevent destabilizing shifts.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.3 </span>Score-Based Skill Pool Maintenance

<div id="S4.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Since the Skill pool has a fixed capacity $`K`$, indiscriminately adding
new Skills increases storage and selection overhead. Beyond PPO Gate, we
retain or prune Skills based on their empirical contribution, measured
by an online score. During interaction, multiple Skills may be invoked
within a trajectory, and rewards may be provided at different
granularities. We therefore define a unified, advantage-style Skill
gain. Given a trajectory $`\tau`$ with rewards $`\{r_{t}\}`$, we first
form a per-step advantage signal
$`\tilde{r}_{t}\triangleq r_{t}-\bar{r}`$, where $`\bar{r}`$ is a
running baseline. The gain of Skill $`\omega`$ in $`\tau`$ is defined as
the average advantage accumulated over the time steps during which
$`\omega`$ is active:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
G(\omega;\tau)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{T}_{\omega}(\tau)|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{\omega}(\tau)}\tilde{r}_{t},
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(7)</span> |

where $`\mathcal{T}_{\omega}(\tau)`$ denotes the set of time steps when
Skill $`\omega`$ is executed. When only a trajectory-level return
$`R(\tau)`$ is available, we set
$`\tilde{r}_{t}\equiv(R(\tau)-\bar{R})/|\tau|`$, yielding the same
advantage-style definition.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.p2.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Online score
update.</span> For each Skill $`\omega`$, we maintain a cumulative gain
$`G_{b}(\omega)`$ and an invocation count $`N_{b}(\omega)`$. After
processing a batch $`\mathcal{T}^{(b)}`$, we update the online score:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle G_{b+1}=G_{b}+`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum G(\omega;\tau),\qquad N_{b+1}=N_{b}+\sum c(\omega;\tau),`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\text{Score}_{b+1}=\frac{G_{b+1}}{\max(1,N_{b+1})}.`$ |  |

<span id="S4.SS3.p2.5.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Online
Score-Based Pruning.</span> To enforce the fixed pool capacity, we
maintain the Skill pool using online scores. Specifically, we remove (i)
Skills with non-positive online score, i.e.,
$`\text{Score}(\omega)\leq 0`$, which indicates no expected advantage
over existing Skills, and (ii) duplicate or semantically redundant
Skills. If the pool still exceeds capacity, we further prune Skills in
ascending order of online score. As the baseline improves over time,
this rule imposes evolutionary pressure, phasing out obsolete Skills
while retaining those with consistently positive gains.

</div>

class="math inline">Mean<sub>±Std Dev</sub></span>. Unit types in “Avg
Tokens Per Unit” denote T: Trajectory, I: Insights, N: Notes, W:
Workflow, PT: Part of Trajectory, and Skill (ours). <span
class="math inline">↑</span> (<span class="math inline">↓</span>)
indicates higher (lower) is better. </span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Experiments

<div id="S5.p1" class="ltx_para">

To examine whether ProcMEM learns reusable procedural memory
(instantiated as Skills) from interaction experience, we conduct the
following experiments:

- <span id="S5.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RQ1:</span>
  Is the learned procedural memory efficiently reusable?
  (§ <a href="#S5.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
  title="5.2 Does ProcMEM Truly Enable Reusability? ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.2</span></a>)

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RQ2:</span>
  How do different components contribute to learning reusable procedural
  memory? (§ <a href="#S5.SS3" class="ltx_ref"
  title="5.3 Why Does ProcMEM Work? ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.3</span></a>)

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RQ3:</span>
  How does procedural memory evolve and get reused in practice?
  (§ <a href="#S5.SS4" class="ltx_ref"
  title="5.4 How Does ProcMEM Evolve and Reuse? ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.4</span></a>)

  </div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.1 </span>Experimental Setup

<div id="S5.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Benchmarks.</span> We conduct experiments
on <span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ALFWorld</span>
 (Shridhar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref"
title="ALFWorld: Aligning Text and Embodied Environments for Interactive Learning">2021</a>)
and <span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">TextArena</span> (Guertler<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref" title="TextArena">2025</a>), two
canonical benchmarks for multi-turn sequential decision-making. ALFWorld
separates training from out-of-distribution environments, while
<span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Mastermind-v0</span> from TextArena spans
three difficulty tiers; both supporting evaluation of cross-task memory
reuse (see Appendix <a href="#A2.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="B.1 Benchmarks ‣ Appendix B Detailed Experimental Setup ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B.1</span></a>).

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Baselines.</span> We compare ProcMEM
against a diverse set of memory-augmented and reasoning-based LLM
agents. Memory-augmented baselines span raw trajectory retrieval
(<span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RAG</span> (Lewis<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref"
title="Retrieval-augmented generation for knowledge-intensive nlp tasks">2020</a>)),
distilled insights (<span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Expel</span> (Zhao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref"
title="Expel: llm agents are experiential learners">2024</a>)), concise
notes (<span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">A-MEM</span> (Xu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref"
title="A-mem: agentic memory for llm agents">2025</a>)), structured
workflows (<span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">AWM</span> (Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref"
title="Agent workflow memory">2024</a>)), and hybrid memory
representations (<span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">G-Memory</span> (Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref"
title="G-memory: tracing hierarchical memory for multi-agent systems">2025a</a>)).
We further include representative reasoning-based baselines, including
<span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ReAct</span> (Yao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref"
title="React: synergizing reasoning and acting in language models">2022</a>)
and <span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.8"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">CoT</span> (Wei<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib54" class="ltx_ref"
title="Chain-of-thought prompting elicits reasoning in large language models">2022</a>),
as well as a <span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.9"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">State</span>-based agent without external
memory. All methods use the same frozen LLM, ensuring a fair comparison
without parameter fine-tuning.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">LLM
Backbones.</span> We evaluate ProcMEM with multiple LLM backbones. On
TextArena, we learn Skills using <span id="S5.SS1.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Gemma-2-9B</span> and evaluate
their reuse across heterogeneous agents, including
<span id="S5.SS1.p3.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Gemma-3-4B</span>,
<span id="S5.SS1.p3.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Qwen3-32B</span>, and
<span id="S5.SS1.p3.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">LLaMA-3.3-70B-Instruct</span>. On
ALFWorld, all experiments are conducted with <span id="S5.SS1.p3.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Qwen3-32B</span>.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Evaluation
Metrics.</span> We evaluate all methods along three complementary
dimensions: <span id="S5.SS1.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(1)
Memory reuse</span> is measured by <span id="S5.SS1.p4.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">in-domain, cross-task, and cross-agent
reuse rates</span>, indicating probability that stored memory is reused
per episode. <span id="S5.SS1.p4.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(2)
Performance</span> is measured by an agent’s episodic return.
<span id="S5.SS1.p4.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(3)
Efficiency</span> evaluates the *storage cost* and *inference cost* of
memory reuse, measured by total tokens stored in memory (Total Stored
Tokens), average tokens per memory units (Avg Tokens per Unit),
additional prompt tokens added to the decision prompt ($`\Delta`$ Prompt
Tokens/Step), and the probability of retrieving memory at each step
(Retrieval Ratio). Metric details are provided in
Appendix <a href="#A2.SS4" class="ltx_ref"
title="B.4 Evaluation Metrics ‣ Appendix B Detailed Experimental Setup ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B.4</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.2 </span>Does ProcMEM Truly Enable Reusability?

<div id="S5.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate ProcMEM and baseline memory methods on memory reuse and
efficiency (Table <a href="#S4.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ 4.3 Score-Based Skill Pool Maintenance ‣ 4 Non-Parametric PPO for Skill Evolution ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>), as well as task performance
(Table <a href="#S4.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 4.3 Score-Based Skill Pool Maintenance ‣ 4 Non-Parametric PPO for Skill Evolution ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>). Memories are built on
in-domain tasks (Mastermind-v0 and ALFWorld-Train) and reused on
out-of-distribution or higher-difficulty tasks (cross-task), and across
agents with different LLM backbones (cross-agent); results are averaged
over 50 episodes per setting.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ProcMEM’s
superior reuse rates validate both Skill-MDP effectiveness and learned
Skills’ quality.</span> As shown in
Table <a href="#S4.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ 4.3 Score-Based Skill Pool Maintenance ‣ 4 Non-Parametric PPO for Skill Evolution ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, ProcMEM’s reuse rate
consistently outperforms all baselines in in-domain, cross-task, and
cross-agent evaluations. While baselines suffer from low reuse due to
redundant episodic data, ProcMEM’s high reuse rate demonstrates that
learned procedural Skills are both high-quality and inherently
generalizable.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ProcMEM’s high
efficiency is evidenced by minimal storage and low execution
overhead.</span> While baselines accumulate hundreds of thousands of
tokens by storing diverse episodic units, such as trajectories,
insights, and workflows, ProcMEM maintains only <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">816 tokens</span>, demonstrating that
procedural memory is far more compact than episodic memory. ProcMEM’s
lean representation, as reflected by $`\Delta`$ Prompt Tokens/Step,
prevents prompt bloat and minimizes LLM execution load. Furthermore, by
utilizing temporally extended “Skills”, ProcMEM reduces per-step
retrieval ratio, ensuring highly efficient agent execution.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ProcMEM achieves
superior performance despite a highly compressed memory
footprint.</span> As shown in Table <a href="#S4.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 4.3 Score-Based Skill Pool Maintenance ‣ 4 Non-Parametric PPO for Skill Evolution ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>, our learned memory
consistently yields performance gains when reused across varying task
difficulties and LLM backbones of different scales. Notably, even under
extreme memory compression, ProcMEM maintains the highest success rates,
reaching <span id="S5.SS2.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">0.90
</span>in ALFWorld. This superior performance confirms that our
framework successfully captures essential task logic, ensuring that only
high-quality, decision-critical content is stored.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.3 </span>Why Does ProcMEM Work?

<div id="S5.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

To evaluate the contribution of each component, we conduct an ablation
study comparing ProcMEM against several variants, primarily using the
Mastermind-v0 environment in TextArena. Beyond performance and reuse
rate, we introduce two metrics: <span id="S5.SS3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Online Score</span> (average Skill
quality in the pool) and <span id="S5.SS3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">PPO Gate Pass Rate</span> (the ratio of
candidates satisfying PPO Gate).

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="S5.I2.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I2.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I2.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">w/o
  Skill</span>: Utilizes only states for decision-making.

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I2.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I2.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I2.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">w/o
  NP-PPO</span>: Employs fixed skill seeds without the NP-PPO evolution
  process.

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I2.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I2.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I2.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">w/o
  SG</span>: Replaces Semantic Gradients with trajectory summaries;
  directly utilizing raw trajectories would otherwise trigger context
  window overflow for <span id="S5.I2.i3.p1.1.2"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Gemma-2-9B</span>.

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I2.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I2.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I2.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">w/o PPO
  Gate</span>: Removes the PPO Gate, allowing all generated candidates
  to enter the skill pool unconditionally.

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I2.i5"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I2.i5.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I2.i5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">w/o Score
  (FIFO)</span>: Replaces score-based pruning with a First-In-First-Out
  (FIFO) to manage pool capacity.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS3.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Both the
procedural Skill and NP-PPO evolution are fundamental to task
success.</span> As shown in Table <a href="#S5.T3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 3 ‣ 5.3 Why Does ProcMEM Work? ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>, the <span id="S5.SS3.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">w/o Skill</span> variant suffers a sharp
performance drop ($`0.606\rightarrow 0.388`$),confirming that skills are
essential building blocks for complex decision-making. While initial
seeds provide a functional baseline, the <span id="S5.SS3.p3.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">w/o NP-PPO</span> results highlight that
our evolution mechanism is critical for refining general seeds into
task-specific expertise, significantly boosting both reuse and success
rates.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS3.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Semantic
Gradients and the PPO Gate are indispensable for the generation and
verification of high-quality Skills.</span> Ablating either component
degrades pool quality. Specifically, <span id="S5.SS3.p4.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">w/o SG</span> triggers a 30% drop in the
PPO Gate Pass Rate, proving that Semantic Gradients significantly
enhance the quality of generated skill candidates. Conversely,
<span id="S5.SS3.p4.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">w/o PPO
Gate</span> admits all candidates without verification, destabilizing
training as evidenced in the training curves
(Fig. <a href="#S5.F3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 3 ‣ 5.3 Why Does ProcMEM Work? ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>). Notably,
<span id="S5.SS3.p4.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">w/o SG</span>
remains more stable than <span id="S5.SS3.p4.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">w/o PPO Gate</span>, as its candidates
must still pass trust-region verification.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p5" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS3.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Score-based
maintenance is critical for preserving evolutionary gains within the
skill pool.</span> The <span id="S5.SS3.p5.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">w/o Score (FIFO)</span> variant exhibits
the most severe degradation among all NP-PPO ablations. Despite
maintaining a high PPO Gate Pass Rate, FIFO inadvertently replaces
high-performing Skills with unproven newcomers. The resulting negative
Online Score ($`-0.0018`$) confirms that without score-based pruning,
the pool fails to retain superior procedural knowledge, ultimately
leading to a collapse in long-term performance.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.4 </span>How Does ProcMEM Evolve and Reuse?

<figure id="S5.F4" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<img src="/html/2602.01869/assets/x3.png" id="S5.F4.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_figure_panel ltx_img_landscape"
width="788" height="269" alt="Refer to caption" />
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_break">

</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">
<img src="/html/2602.01869/assets/x4.png" id="S5.F4.g2"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_figure_panel ltx_img_landscape"
width="789" height="261" alt="Refer to caption" />
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F4.5.2.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 4</span>: </span><span
id="S5.F4.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">Evolutionary Lineage of Skills.<span
id="S5.F4.2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Gray bars represent
Skill lifespans along the evolutionary timeline (horizontal axis).
Dashed vertical lines denote refinement events where a parent Skill
evolves into children; multiple lines indicate repeated refinements. Red
‘X’ markers signify pruning of underperforming variants for pool
efficiency. The dark blue arrow and sequential alignment (e.g., <span
class="math inline"><em>v</em><sub>1</sub> → <em>v</em><sub>13</sub></span>)
track the sustained trajectory of Skill
evolution.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

<figure id="S5.F5" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2602.01869/assets/x5.png" id="S5.F5.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="483" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F5.6.2.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 5</span>: </span><span
id="S5.F5.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"
style="font-size:90%;">Skill distribution across LLM agents and task
complexities.<span id="S5.F5.2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium">
Bars represent the empirical invocation probability for each skill
categorized by different LLM backbones (top) and task difficulty levels
in <span id="S5.F5.2.1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Mastermind-v0</span> (bottom). <span
class="math inline"><em>N</em></span> denotes the average number of
skill invocations per episode.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Cross-Agent and
Cross-Task Generalization.</span> Fig. <a href="#S5.F5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 5 ‣ 5.4 How Does ProcMEM Evolve and Reuse? ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> characterizes skill reuse
through invocation probability and mean frequency per episode ($`N`$).
Our analysis reveals that while different LLM backbones exhibit distinct
usage profiles, the underlying selection patterns remain remarkably
stable across varying task difficulties. Specifically, in cross-agent
evaluations, <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Gemma2-9B</span> shows a heightened
reliance on <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">FBInference</span>, whereas
<span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">StratPlan</span> maintains
consistent activation levels across agents, acting as a standardized
procedural primitive. Crucially, in cross-task evaluations on
<span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Mastermind-v0</span>, the skill
distribution remains invariant as difficulty scales from Base to
Extreme. This consistency demonstrates that ProcMEM effectively distills
the fundamental task logic, enabling robust generalization across
environment complexities.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Evolutionary
Dynamics.</span> Fig. <a href="#S5.F4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 4 ‣ 5.4 How Does ProcMEM Evolve and Reuse? ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> illustrates the evolutionary
trajectory of two representative Skills. The observed refinements and
score-based pruning events underscore ProcMEM’s ability to evolve a
compact, high-utility skill pool. Detailed analysis is provided in
Appendix <a href="#A3.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="C.1 Evolutionary Lineage Analysis. ‣ Appendix C Additional Experimental Details ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.1</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">6 </span>Conclusion

<div id="S6.p1" class="ltx_para">

We presented ProcMEM, a framework enabling LLM agents to
<span id="S6.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">autonomously learn
procedural memory</span> without parameter updates. By formalizing the
<span id="S6.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Skill-MDP</span>,
ProcMEM transforms passive episodic narratives into executable, reusable
Skills, eliminating redundant on-the-fly reasoning. To ensure
reliability without capability degradation, we propose
<span id="S6.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Non-Parametric
PPO</span>, which leverages semantic gradients for high-quality
candidate generation and a PPO Gate for robust Skill verification.
Finally, a score-based maintenance mechanism prunes low-return Skills to
sustain long-term memory quality.

</div>

<div id="S6.p2" class="ltx_para">

Results across diverse scenarios confirm that ProcMEM achieves
<span id="S6.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">superior reuse rates
and significant performance gains with extreme memory
compression</span>. Our findings validate that <span id="S6.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">high-quality procedural memory is
fundamentally more efficient than raw episodic storage</span> for
long-term autonomy. Future work will integrate implicit execution
modules to better emulate human-like intelligence. Ultimately, the
autonomous accumulation of procedural expertise via interaction without
parameter updates represents a pivotal milestone toward the emergence of
truly self-evolving artificial intelligence.

</div>

</div>

<div id="Sx1" class="section ltx_section">

## Impact Statement

<div id="Sx1.p1" class="ltx_para">

This paper presents work whose goal is to advance the field of machine
learning by improving the efficiency and reusability of autonomous
agents. By enabling procedural knowledge accumulation without continuous
parameter updates, our work contributes to more resource-efficient and
sustainable AI development. There are many potential societal
consequences of our work, none of which we feel must be specifically
highlighted here.

</div>

</div>
