<span class="ltx_rule"
style="width:100%;height:1px;--ltx-bg-color:black;display:inline-block;"> </span>

<figure id="alg1"
class="ltx_float ltx_float_algorithm ltx_framed ltx_framed_top">
<div id="alg1.3" class="ltx_listing">
<div id="alg1.l1" class="ltx_listingline">
 <span id="alg1.l1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Input:</span>
Initial Skill pool <span
class="math inline"><em>Ω</em><sub>0</sub></span>, Frozen LLM <span
class="math inline"><em>π</em><sub>LLM</sub></span>, Capacity <span
class="math inline"><em>K</em></span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l2" class="ltx_listingline">
 Initialize online scores <span
class="math inline">Score(<em>ω</em>) = 0</span> for all <span
class="math inline"><em>ω</em> ∈ <em>Ω</em><sub>0</sub></span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l3" class="ltx_listingline">
 <span id="alg1.l3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">for</span> <span
class="math inline"><em>n</em> = 1</span> <span id="alg1.l3.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">to</span> <span
class="math inline"><em>N</em></span> <span id="alg1.l3.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">do</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l4" class="ltx_listingline">
  <span id="alg1.l4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">// 1. Experience
Collection</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l5" class="ltx_listingline">
  Collect a batch of trajectories <span
class="math inline">𝒯<sup>(<em>B</em>)</sup></span> using policy <span
class="math inline"><em>π</em><sub><em>Ω</em></sub> = <em>μ</em> ⋅ <em>π</em><sub>LLM</sub></span>.
</div>
<div id="alg1.l6" class="ltx_listingline">
  <span id="alg1.l6.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">// 2. Semantic
Gradient Extraction &amp; Optimization</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l7" class="ltx_listingline">
  <span id="alg1.l7.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">for</span> each
Skill <span
class="math inline"><em>ω</em> ∈ <em>Ω</em><sub><em>n</em></sub></span>
invoked in <span class="math inline">𝒯<sup>(<em>B</em>)</sup></span>
<span id="alg1.l7.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">do</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l8" class="ltx_listingline">
   Extract per-trajectory semantic gradients <span
class="math inline">{<em>g</em><sub><em>i</em></sub>}</span> via
hindsight attribution.
</div>
<div id="alg1.l9" class="ltx_listingline">
   <span
class="math inline"><em>ḡ</em><sub><em>ω</em></sub> = Aggregate({<em>g</em><sub><em>i</em></sub>}<sub><em>i</em> = 1</sub><sup><em>B</em></sup>)</span>
{Batch-level aggregation}
</div>
<div id="alg1.l10" class="ltx_listingline">
   Generate <span
class="math inline"><em>N</em><sub><em>c</em></sub></span> candidates
<span
class="math inline">{<em>ω</em><sub><em>j</em></sub><sup>′</sup>}</span>
where <span
class="math inline"><em>ω</em><sup>′</sup> = <em>ω</em> ⊕ <em>ḡ</em><sub><em>ω</em></sub></span>
via LLM.
</div>
<div id="alg1.l11" class="ltx_listingline">
   <span id="alg1.l11.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">// 3. PPO-Style
Trust-Region Verification (PPO Gate)</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l12" class="ltx_listingline">
   <span id="alg1.l12.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">for</span> each
candidate <span
class="math inline"><em>ω</em><sub><em>j</em></sub><sup>′</sup></span>
<span id="alg1.l12.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">do</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l13" class="ltx_listingline">
    Compute <span
class="math inline">$J(\omega^{\prime}_{j})=\hat{\mathbb{E}}[\min(\rho_{t}\hat{A}_{t},\text{clip}(\rho_{t},1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)\hat{A}_{t})]$</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l14" class="ltx_listingline">
   <span id="alg1.l14.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">end</span> <span
id="alg1.l14.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">for</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l15" class="ltx_listingline">
   <span
class="math inline"><em>ω</em><sup>*</sup> = arg max<sub><em>ω</em><sub><em>j</em></sub><sup>′</sup></sub><em>J</em>(<em>ω</em><sub><em>j</em></sub><sup>′</sup>)</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l16" class="ltx_listingline">
   <span id="alg1.l16.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">if</span> <span
class="math inline"><em>J</em>(<em>ω</em><sup>*</sup>) &gt; 0</span>
<span id="alg1.l16.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">then</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l17" class="ltx_listingline">
    <span
class="math inline"><em>Ω</em> = <em>Ω</em> ∪ {<em>ω</em><sup>*</sup>}</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l18" class="ltx_listingline">
   <span id="alg1.l18.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">end</span> <span
id="alg1.l18.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">if</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l19" class="ltx_listingline">
  <span id="alg1.l19.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">end</span> <span
id="alg1.l19.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">for</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l20" class="ltx_listingline">
  <span id="alg1.l20.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">// 4. Skill Pool
Maintenance</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l21" class="ltx_listingline">
  Update <span class="math inline">Score(<em>ω</em>)</span> based on
cumulative gain <span
class="math inline"><em>G</em>(<em>ω</em>; <em>τ</em>)</span>.
</div>
<div id="alg1.l22" class="ltx_listingline">
  <span id="alg1.l22.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">if</span> <span
class="math inline">|<em>Ω</em>| &gt; <em>K</em></span> <span
id="alg1.l22.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">then</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l23" class="ltx_listingline">
   Prune Skills with <span
class="math inline">Score(<em>ω</em>) ≤ 0</span> or those with lowest
scores, semantically redundant items via cosine similarity.
</div>
<div id="alg1.l24" class="ltx_listingline">
  <span id="alg1.l24.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">end</span> <span
id="alg1.l24.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">if</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l25" class="ltx_listingline">
 <span id="alg1.l25.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">end</span> <span
id="alg1.l25.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">for</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l26" class="ltx_listingline">
 <span id="alg1.l26.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Output:</span>
Optimized Skill pool <span
class="math inline"><em>Ω</em><sub><em>N</em></sub></span>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_float"><span id="alg1.2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Algorithm 1</span> </span> Non-Parametric
PPO for Skill Evolution</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="A1" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix A </span>Full Related Works

<div id="A1.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Learning from
Interaction Experience in LLM Agents.</span> Recent LLM-agent frameworks
improve sequential decision making by leveraging interaction experience,
either through *parametric fine-tuning* or via *non-parametric*
adaptation at inference time. Parametric methods, such as reinforcement
learning (RL), incorporate feedback from interaction by updating model
parameters and have demonstrated strong task performance
(Ouyang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref"
title="Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback">2022</a>;
Rafailov<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref"
title="Direct preference optimization: your language model is secretly a reward model">2023</a>;
Guo<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref"
title="DeepSeek-r1 incentivizes reasoning in llms through reinforcement learning">2025</a>).
However, as pretrained LLMs become increasingly capable general-purpose
reasoners, task-specific fine-tuning is often *computationally
expensive*, tends to *over-specialize models to narrow task
distributions*, and can *degrade general-purpose behaviors* under
continual adaptation (Ziegler<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref"
title="Fine-tuning language models from human preferences">2020</a>;
Shi<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref"
title="Continual learning of large language models: a comprehensive survey">2025a</a>;
Luo<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref"
title="An empirical study of catastrophic forgetting in large language models during continual fine-tuning">2025</a>).
These limitations have motivated growing interest in *non-parametric*
approaches that learn from interaction experience *without updating
model parameters*, among which memory-augmented LLM agents constitute a
dominant paradigm (Zhao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref"
title="Expel: llm agents are experiential learners">2024</a>;
Zhou<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memento: fine-tuning llm agents without fine-tuning llms">2025a</a>).

</div>

<div id="A1.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Memory-Augmented LLM
Agents.</span> Memory-augmented LLM agents store past interaction
experience in an external memory and retrieve relevant content to
condition the LLM’s reasoning during decision making, thereby extending
the agent’s effective temporal horizon without updating model parameters
(Zhao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref"
title="Expel: llm agents are experiential learners">2024</a>;
Yang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref"
title="Learning on the job: an experience-driven self-evolving agent for long-horizon tasks">2025</a>;
Cai<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref"
title="Flex: continuous agent evolution via forward learning from experience">2025</a>).
Existing methods mainly differ in what experience is stored, how it is
retrieved, and how the memory is updated over time. The most basic form
stores <span id="A1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">raw
trajectories or episodic records</span> and retrieves full or partial
past episodes to guide current decisions in a case-based manner
(Park<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref"
title="Generative agents: interactive simulacra of human behavior">2023</a>).
To improve efficiency and generalization, several approaches distill
experience into <span id="A1.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">abstract summaries</span>
(Yang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref"
title="Learning on the job: an experience-driven self-evolving agent for long-horizon tasks">2025</a>),
<span id="A1.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">high-level
principles</span> (Wu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref"
title="EvolveR: self-evolving llm agents through an experience-driven lifecycle">2025</a>;
Agrawal<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref"
title="Gepa: reflective prompt evolution can outperform reinforcement learning">2025</a>;
Cai<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref"
title="Flex: continuous agent evolution via forward learning from experience">2025</a>),
or <span id="A1.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">insights</span>
extracted from past successes or failures
(Zhao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref"
title="Expel: llm agents are experiential learners">2024</a>). To
capture complex dependencies across long horizons, <span id="A1.p2.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">structured or graph-based memory</span>
organizes experience into hierarchical or graph representations, such as
G-Memory (Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref"
title="G-memory: tracing hierarchical memory for multi-agent systems">2025a</a>)
and HippocRAG (Jimenez Gutierrez<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref"
title="Hipporag: neurobiologically inspired long-term memory for large language models">2024</a>),
enabling multi-hop retrieval and reasoning
(Rezazadeh<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref"
title="From isolated conversations to hierarchical schemas: dynamic tree memory representation for llms">2024</a>;
Xia<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref"
title="From experience to strategy: empowering llm agents with trainable graph memory">2025</a>).
In parallel, <span id="A1.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">dense
vector compression</span> encodes experience into latent embeddings or
matrices to support scalable storage and similarity-based retrieval, as
in LARIMAR (Das<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref"
title="Larimar: large language models with episodic memory control">2024</a>)
and MemGen (Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memgen: weaving generative latent memory for self-evolving agents">2025b</a>).
More recent work maintains <span id="A1.p2.1.8"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">dynamic knowledge snippets</span>, such
as textual notes or discrete knowledge units inspired by human
note-taking, which are continuously updated during interaction,
including Self-RAG (Asai<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref"
title="Self-rag: learning to retrieve, generate, and critique through self-reflection">2024</a>),
ReMemR1 (Shi<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref"
title="Look back to reason forward: revisitable memory for long-context llm agents">2025b</a>),
MemGen (Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memgen: weaving generative latent memory for self-evolving agents">2025b</a>),
and Mem1 (Zhou<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref"
title="MEM1: learning to synergize memory and reasoning for efficient long-horizon agents">2025b</a>).
Finally, some approaches store explicit task-completion paths or
workflows that can be retrieved to guide future actions
(Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref"
title="Agent workflow memory">2024</a>). Despite these advances,
existing memory-augmented agents prioritize experience storage over
content reusability. As interaction trajectories grow, this paradigm
inevitably accumulates massive redundancy, leading to prohibitive
storage and retrieval overhead. Furthermore, treating retrieved episodes
as passive context forces agents to repetitively re-reason actions
within limited context windows, imposing significant inference pressure.

</div>

<div id="A1.p3" class="ltx_para">

Relatedly, skill-based agents (Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Voyager: an open-ended embodied agent with large language models">2023</a>;
Tan<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Cradle: empowering foundation agents towards general computer control">2024</a>)
and procedural knowledge acquisition
(Zhu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref"
title="Ghost in the minecraft: generally capable agents for open-world environments via large language models with text-based knowledge and memory">2023</a>;
Sumers<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Cognitive architectures for language agents">2023</a>) explore
capturing executable behaviors. Recent studies
(Han<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Legomem: modular procedural memory for multi-agent llm systems for workflow automation">2025</a>;
Fang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memp: exploring agent procedural memory">2025</a>) have pioneered
procedural memory mechanisms, yet optimizing execution reusability
remains an open problem. To bridge this gap, we propose ProcMEM to
formalize and learn reusable procedural memory from interaction
experience, ensuring efficient and reliable long-term autonomy.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix B </span>Detailed Experimental Setup

<div id="A2.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">B.1 </span>Benchmarks

<div id="A2.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate ProcMEM on <span id="A2.SS1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">TextArena</span> (Guertler<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref" title="TextArena">2025</a>) and
<span id="A2.SS1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ALFWorld</span> (Shridhar<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref"
title="ALFWorld: Aligning Text and Embodied Environments for Interactive Learning">2021</a>),
two benchmarks that capture the core challenges of experience reuse in
sequential decision-making. TextArena consists of multi-turn, text-based
games with varying levels of difficulty. These tasks require
long-horizon reasoning and adaptation to iterative feedback, making them
well suited for evaluating repeated reuse of accumulated experience both
within and across tasks. ALFWorld is an embodied environment grounded in
natural language, involving long action sequences and high-level
decision-making in an abstract state space. Importantly, ALFWorld
explicitly separates training tasks from out-of-distribution evaluation
tasks, enabling direct assessment of experience reuse under distribution
shift.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">B.2 </span>Baselines

<div id="A2.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We compare ProcMEM against a comprehensive set of memory-augmented and
reasoning-based LLM agents. Memory-augmented baselines differ in how
experience is stored, including raw interaction trajectories
(<span id="A2.SS2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RAG</span>) (Lewis<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref"
title="Retrieval-augmented generation for knowledge-intensive nlp tasks">2020</a>),
distilled insights (<span id="A2.SS2.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Expel</span>) (Zhao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref"
title="Expel: llm agents are experiential learners">2024</a>), concise
notes (<span id="A2.SS2.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">A-MEM</span>) (Xu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref"
title="A-mem: agentic memory for llm agents">2025</a>), structured
workflows (<span id="A2.SS2.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">AWM</span>) (Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref"
title="Agent workflow memory">2024</a>), and hybrid memory
representations (<span id="A2.SS2.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">G-Memory</span>) (Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref"
title="G-memory: tracing hierarchical memory for multi-agent systems">2025a</a>).
All of these methods retrieve past experience to condition
decision-making. We additionally include representative reasoning-based
baselines, including <span id="A2.SS2.p1.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ReAct</span> (Yao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref"
title="React: synergizing reasoning and acting in language models">2022</a>)
with chain-of-thought (<span id="A2.SS2.p1.1.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">CoT</span>) (Wei<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib54" class="ltx_ref"
title="Chain-of-thought prompting elicits reasoning in large language models">2022</a>)
reasoning, as well as a minimal <span id="A2.SS2.p1.1.8"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">State</span>-based agent that directly
selects actions from the current environment state without external
memory. All methods use the same frozen LLM for decision-making, with no
parameter fine-tuning, ensuring a fair and controlled comparison.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">B.3 </span>LLM Backbones

<div id="A2.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

To evaluate robustness across model scales and architectures, we conduct
experiments with multiple LLM backbones. On TextArena, the main
experiments are performed using <span id="A2.SS3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Gemma-2-9B</span>, and the
resulting Skill pool is reused across heterogeneous LLM agents,
including <span id="A2.SS3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Gemma-3-4B</span>,
<span id="A2.SS3.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Qwen3-32B</span>, and
<span id="A2.SS3.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">LLaMA-3.3-70B-Instruct</span>, to
assess cross-agent reuse efficiency and performance. On ALFWorld, all
experiments are conducted using <span id="A2.SS3.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Qwen3-32B</span>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">B.4 </span>Evaluation Metrics

<div id="A2.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate all methods along three complementary dimensions: *task
performance*, *memory reuse*, and *efficiency*. <span id="A2.SS4.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(1) Task performance</span> measures an
agent’s ability to solve sequential decision-making tasks.
<span id="A2.SS4.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(2) Experience
reuse</span> is quantified by <span id="A2.SS4.p1.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">in-domain, cross-task, and cross-agent
reuse rates</span>, capturing how effectively stored memory is reused
across tasks and LLM backbones. <span id="A2.SS4.p1.1.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(3) Efficiency</span> measures the cost
of memory reuse, including storage footprint and inference-time
overhead, quantified by total stored tokens, average tokens per memory
unit, retrieval ratio, and additional prompt tokens per step.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A2.SS4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(1) Task
Performance.</span> We report standard benchmark-specific performance
metrics. For TextArena environments, we use the average return per
episode. For ALFWorld, we report success rate, which directly reflects
task completion performance.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS4.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A2.SS4.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(2) Reuse
Metrics.</span> To quantify how effectively stored experience is reused,
we introduce three reuse metrics. Let $`\mathcal{M}`$ denote the set of
stored units.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS4.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A2.SS4.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">In-domain Reuse
Rate ($`\uparrow`$).</span> This metric measures how much stored
experience is actually reused within the same task domain. It is defined
as the fraction of stored units that are invoked at least once during
evaluation:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\text{In-domain Reuse Rate}=\frac{\left|\{u\in\mathcal{M}\mid\text{used}(u)\geq 1\}\right|}{\left|\mathcal{M}\right|}.
``` |  |

</div>

<div id="A2.SS4.p5" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A2.SS4.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Cross-task Reuse
Rate ($`\uparrow`$).</span> This metric evaluates whether experience
generalizes across tasks. It is defined as the fraction of stored units
that are reused in target tasks different from those in which they were
learned:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\text{Cross-task Reuse Rate}=\frac{\left|\{u\in\mathcal{M}\mid\exists\,\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\text{target}},\ \text{used}_{\tau}(u)\geq 1\}\right|}{\left|\mathcal{M}\right|}.
``` |  |

</div>

<div id="A2.SS4.p6" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A2.SS4.p6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Cross-agent
Reuse Rate ($`\uparrow`$).</span> This metric measures whether stored
experience can be reused across different LLM backbones. It is defined
as the fraction of stored units that are reused by at least one
alternative LLM agent:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\text{Cross-agent Reuse Rate}=\frac{\left|\{u\in\mathcal{M}\mid\exists\,a\in\mathcal{A},\ \text{used}_{a}(u)\geq 1\}\right|}{\left|\mathcal{M}\right|},
``` |  |

where $`\mathcal{A}`$ denotes the set of evaluated LLM agents.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS4.p7" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A2.SS4.p7.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(3) Efficiency
Metrics.</span> Beyond reuse effectiveness, we measure the efficiency of
experience storage and reuse, including both memory footprint and
inference-time overhead.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS4.p8" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A2.SS4.p8.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Total Stored
Tokens ($`\downarrow`$).</span> This metric quantifies the overall
storage footprint by summing the token counts of all stored units:

|     |                                                                    |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                                                    
       ``` math                                                            
       \text{Total Stored Tokens}=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}}\text{tokens}(u).  
       ```                                                                 |     |

</div>

<div id="A2.SS4.p9" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A2.SS4.p9.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Avg Tokens per
Unit ($`\downarrow`$).</span> This metric measures representation
compactness and is defined as the average token length per stored unit:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\text{Avg Tokens per Unit}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}}\text{tokens}(u).
``` |  |

</div>

<div id="A2.SS4.p10" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A2.SS4.p10.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Retrieval Ratio
($`\downarrow`$).</span> This metric measures how frequently experience
reuse is triggered during decision-making. It is defined as the fraction
of decision steps in which a stored unit is retrieved or activated:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\text{Retrieval Ratio}=\frac{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{I}[\text{reuse}_{t}]}{T},
``` |  |

where $`\mathbb{I}[\cdot]`$ is an indicator function and $`T`$ is the
total number of decision steps.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS4.p11" class="ltx_para">

$`\Delta`$<span id="A2.SS4.p11.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Prompt Tokens / Step
($`\downarrow`$).</span> This metric captures the additional inference
burden introduced by experience reuse. It is defined as the average
increase in prompt tokens relative to a state-only prompt:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\Delta\text{Prompt Tokens / Step}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Big(\text{tokens}(\text{prompt}_{t})-\text{tokens}(\text{state}_{t})\Big).
``` |  |

For comparable task performance, a lower value indicates reduced
inference overhead and less reliance on large contextual inputs.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">C.1 </span>Evolutionary Lineage Analysis.

<div id="A3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Fig. <a href="#S5.F4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 4 ‣ 5.4 How Does ProcMEM Evolve and Reuse? ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ ProcMEM: Learning Reusable Procedural Memory from Experience via Non-Parametric PPO for LLM Agents"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> visualizes the evolutionary
lineage of Skills, offering a transparent view of how Skills are
iteratively refined and consolidated within the Skill Pool. Multiple
vertical dashed links between successive variants—such as
$`v_{2}`$–$`v_{4}`$ of <span id="A3.SS1.p1.3.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">HypothesisElimination</span>—indicate
repeated refinement cycles in which several candidate variants are
temporarily retained until a superior version is validated by online
scores. The frequent appearance of red ‘X’ markers across both
<span id="A3.SS1.p1.3.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">HypothesisElimination</span> and
<span id="A3.SS1.p1.3.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">StrategicPlanning</span> highlights the
critical role of <span id="A3.SS1.p1.3.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">online score–based pruning</span> in
maintaining Skill Pool efficiency, preventing uncontrolled accumulation
of redundant variants that would otherwise degrade performance.
Ultimately, each lineage converges to a persistent Skill (marked by the
dark blue arrow), such as <span id="A3.SS1.p1.3.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">HypothesisElimination</span>
$`v_{12}`$, which remains stable after the exploration phase.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A4" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix D </span>Case Study: Semantic Gradient Generation

<div id="A4.p1" class="ltx_para">

This section illustrates the generation of semantic gradients within the
Mastermind environment. By presenting a representative failure
trajectory, we demonstrate the resulting <span id="A4.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Semantic Gradient</span> and its
corresponding <span id="A4.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Trajectory Summary</span> used in our
ablation studies.

</div>

<div id="A4.p2" class="ltx_para">

The semantic gradient is structured as a tuple of updates for the
Initiation ($`I`$), Policy ($`\pi`$), and Termination ($`\beta`$)
components. Notably, if a component requires no adjustment, its gradient
is represented as an <span id="A4.p2.3.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">empty string</span> (<span id="A4.p2.3.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">""</span>), ensuring that the
evolution remains focused solely on identified errors. For comparison,
we also provide the <span id="A4.p2.3.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Trajectory Summary</span> to highlight
the distinction between neutral, fact-based compression and our proposed
diagnostic gradients.

</div>

<div id="A4.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
